It’s not a mystery 

The site of Al-Aqsa Mosque is viewed as a place of worship with a long history of monotheism. In Islamic belief, all prophets preached the worship of one God, with different laws varying across time, while the core message remained the same.

Islamic narratives indicate that this site was a place of worship for several prophets, which explains its sanctity in Islam. When Al-Aqsa Mosque was built during the Islamic era, no existing religious structure existed on the site, and it was not considered the exclusive religious property of any particular race or group.

Although the site lies within an area considered sacred by Jews in their religious beliefs, the construction of Al-Aqsa Mosque is understood in the Islamic context as a continuation of the site's function as a place of worship, not as the removal or forced replacement of an existing place of worship.

### **@reemelattar3851**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2OdO8LoKuo8&lc=UgzvAKekR6er5_oSM7N4AaABAg)

I hope your wife sees that comment :)

The Prophet Muhammad did not appoint a specific successor because he wanted to avoid creating any special privilege for a particular person or family, which could lead to a monarchy. Instead, the early Muslim community established what was known as Majlis al-Shura, a council of consultation similar to a modern senate, to choose the next leader.

The division between Sunni and Shia Muslims began mainly as a political disagreement. Even though Ali himself did not oppose Abu Bakr, Umar, or any other companion ruling—since they were all close friends—the conflict deepened later. The tragedy in Karbala, where members of Ali’s family were brutally killed, became a turning point. Shia Muslims believe that Muawiya and Yazid were responsible for this, while Sunnis deny that claim, and only Allah truly knows the full truth.

As a Sunni, I deeply feel the sorrow of what happened and regard it as a great tragedy. But I also believe we should not allow it to create new divisions and hatred among Muslims today.

There are also some theological differences: for example, Shia Muslims recognize a line of twelve Imams, while Sunnis follow a different understanding of religious leadership.

Why didn’t Mohammad name a successor: actually he did, it was Abu Bakr, his most loyal Companion and one of the first handful of people to enter Islam. Mohammad had famously married Abu Bakr’s daughter Aisha a long time prior. Think of their friendship as that of Achilles and Patroclus, that’s the level of devotion they had to each other.

Actually, the professor did not do extensive research. The Prophet Muhammad (ﷺ) absolutely appointed a successor, because it was a divine decree required by the Qur’an in Surah Al-Baqarah (2:30).
The Prophet Muhammad (ﷺ) appointed Imam Ali (عليه السلام), his cousin and son-in-law, and this was a divine appointment commanded by Allah. This is mentioned in both Shia and Sunni hadith books, but Sunnis were influenced by politics and followed the companions instead of the rightful successor.
The problem with the Sunni belief that Abu Bakr was the caliph (successor) is that humans are corruptible, and only Allah knows what is in the hearts of people. Abu Bakr, according to Sunni belief, was the closest companion of the Prophet and was selected by a group of people. However, Shias believe it does not work that way. Allah would not leave His religion—sent with truth—to be decided by people as to who represents Islam.
In fact, Umar and Uthman were also chosen differently—some through selection, others through a council. Imam Ali, according to Sunnis, became the fourth caliph by default. All four were chosen using different methods and criteria, which does not make sense.
That is why the Shia position is the only logical one: the Prophet absolutely appointed a successor—Imam Ali (عليه السلام). Look at the event of Ghadir Khumm. It is the clearest evidence. The Prophet left behind the Qur’an and his Ahl al-Bayt (his holy household and progeny), and said that as long as you follow both, you will not be misguided.
Alhamdulillah, the Shias have followed the correct path of the twelve infallible, divinely appointed successors after the Prophet Muhammad (ﷺ), starting with Imam Ali (عليه السلام), followed by Imam Hasan and Imam Husayn (عليهم السلام), his grandsons who sacrificed their lives to preserve Islam from the corruption of Muawiya and Yazid.
This is why divine appointment is a necessity for preserving Islam and the true teachings of the Prophet Muhammad (ﷺ). Shias uphold this truth, and it can be seen even in modern times. Shias in Iran, Lebanon, Yemen, and Iraq are among those actively resisting oppression and supporting the Palestinians, while many Sunni governments—such as Saudi Arabia, Qatar, and Turkey—have taken different political positions.
Not much more needs to be said. Do not listen to propaganda about Shia beliefs. Do your research—the truth is clear.

### **@Cybersecure9999**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2OdO8LoKuo8&lc=UgypbDkby4ezqDoY0ZF4AaABAg)

The early Muslims did not fight the Roman (Byzantine) and Persian empires for random conquest or wealth. The Qur’an permits fighting only in response to aggression and oppression: “Fight those who fight you but do not transgress” (2:190) and “Permission to fight is given to those who are wronged” (22:39). The Prophet ﷺ sent letters peacefully inviting rulers to Islam, but hostility followed—his envoy was killed under Roman authority, and the Persian ruler tore his letter and ordered action against him. Later conflicts happened in the context of military threats and border aggression. The Qur’anic basis remained defense, protection of the oppressed (4:75), and securing freedom of belief—not forced conversion.
The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not clearly name a political successor in the Qur’an or in an explicit, undisputed public declaration. Instead, the Qur’an establishes governance through consultation: “And consult them in affairs” (3:159) and “Their affairs are by mutual consultation” (42:38). During his final illness, he appointed Abu Bakr (RA) to lead the prayer (Sahih Bukhari), which the companions understood as a strong sign of leadership. After his death, leadership was decided through shura among the companions. He also said, “There will be no prophet after me” (Bukhari & Muslim), indicating that political leadership—not prophethood—would continue.

Al-Aqsa Mosque is in Jerusalem because Allah Himself declared that land blessed: “Glory be to the One who took His servant by night from Al-Masjid Al-Haram to Al-Masjid Al-Aqsa whose surroundings We have blessed” (17:1). The Prophet ﷺ also said it was among the first mosques built on earth, forty years after the Ka‘bah (Sahih Bukhari), showing its ancient prophetic origin. It was the first qiblah and one of the three mosques Muslims are encouraged to travel to for worship. Its location is therefore based on divine designation and prophetic legacy, not later political choice.

06:00 That's not the Aqsa mosque but the Dome of the Rock. No one knows if the Aqsa mosque was built on the Temple Mount or if itself is the remains of the Temple Mount. Recall that Muslims used to pray towards Jerusalem first and then to Mecca. Also, Muhammad's Mirage journey happened in his lifetime, which means the Aqsa Mosque had already existed before the Muslims ever conquered Jerusalem.

09:30 Pilgrimage is only for those who are capable, whether financially or in health. But otherwise, it is expected that all Muslims do it at least once (Even the prophet only made the pilgrimage once so there is no need for a Muslim to do it more than once in their lifetime.)

45:00 You are both right yet wrong in many instances. There are plenty of proof of battles where the Companions of the Prophet died in, and then there was the plague that followed the conquests that killed many more. All of these are well documented HOWEVER you are right that many cities and settlements chose to surrender to the Muslims rather than fight due to the more than agreeable terms they were given. The early Islamic conquests were famous for NOT leaving marks of conflicts on the cities; no sackings or razing of cities and most battles were on the open field.

I am curious about which companions you believe were 'purged' and how they were killed.

49:00 Ah, but Muhammad DID name a successor. He said the successor should be counseled among you, basically setting the stage for a sort of Electoral Democracy and the Arabs already had representatives of many tribes and cities in Medina. Abu Bakr was voted to be Caliph and so was Umar (even if he was nominated by Abu Bakr), Uthman, and then Ali. There weren't any controversies in their election, and even Ali's election to the position of Caliph was widely accepted except by Mu'awiya who, while resisted and even rebelled against him, never denounced his position of Caliph.

The successor crisis came LONG after the prophet died and was something that came up much later on by entities clinging to legitimacy.

 Finally, there are two main reasons why Europeans flourished five hundred years ago: Geography. Their geography allowed them easy access to lands easy to conquer to the west and from there, lay the foundation to the European successor states. USA, Brazil, Mexico, Philippines...etc. If it wasn't for the oceans that allowed European settlers to take over half the world, then it wouldn't matter how good their revolutions or modernity is; they would have been stuck in their nations if they didn't have access to the oceans.

All in all, while I do find many things I believe are inherently wrong in your lectures and the information you base your questions on, I still find your perspective on things interesting and enjoyable to watch.

### **@JoJoRock197** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2OdO8LoKuo8&lc=UgwPjPyQFFbdeuIBekR4AaABAg)

Hey Professor, there is literally a Sura (Chapter) in the Quran called Surat Muhammad. He is also named elsewhere in the Quran. His Sira (Biography) is also very well documented and you can find lots of lectures about it on YouTube, as well as plenty of books in the original Arabic (the language in which it would have been written); the Sira is so dense it’s actually several volumes thick; it details his early childhood as an Orphan, the death of his Mother, adoption by his grandfather and later his uncle (who is the father of Ali, to whom Shia pledge loyalty to). There’s plenty of historical record in case you’re looking for it.

My only critique of any Islamic text, is that it was written or compiled AFTER Muhammad’s death, and much of it reads like a mythology (legend) by decades at the very earliest. This INCLUDES the Quran. Only portions of the Quran were written during M’s life, while the majority of it was committed to memory. Following the death of M, and the wars and uprisings that ensued, people who had memorized the entire Quran were getting killed in battle. Additionally, there were new generations of children who never met the prophet M and had no idea why they’re pledging allegiance to anyone or anything. So it was actually about 40 years after Prophet Mohammad’s death that whatever individuals remaining who had committed the Quran to memory, under the supervision of the 4th Caliph Uthman Ibn Affan that the Quran was compiled into a manuscript; the Caliph Uthman having been a loyal companion of M during his lifetime.

You may question the accuracy and completeness of the Quran, and that is a valid issue to present forward (especially if those who had it committed to memory were getting killed in battle/dying of old age); but I will end this point by saying that Muslims pray 5 times a day, and recite the Quran from memory during those prayers.

The period following M’s death was HIGHLY political, so this may or may not have impacted the manuscript if you really want to be a skeptic or genuine in your questioning of historical records.

In my opinion, what is referred to as Hadith can only be highly apocryphal, because it was compiled 500 years (an entire half millennium) after M’s death. The authors and scholars often cite the rigorous standards applied to the sources, but even the best sources with the highest integrity and honesty will fail to tell you whet the had for breakfast yesterday. The Hadith is basically the world’s longest game of telephone ever.

Lastly, the period following M’s death (the Caliphate period, almost 50 years) prior to the formation of the Ummayad empire, was perhaps the most significant and CRITICAL period in the history of Islam; perhaps more critical than the life of the prophet itself. You could potentially make the argument that M focused on a life of spirituality, whereas his descendants/followers where the ones who really launched the political campaigns and reformations, especially during the “apostasy wars” where many people rebelled against Islam, and revealed that they had converted to Islam just to get along, survive, or coexist with Mohammad’s rule while he was still alive.

Make of this what you will, I hope that you find my comments, and that they help you answer some questions, and present forward some new ones.
